### CLAUDE 2026-11-15T12:56

The first step of the game is up and open in the browser pane: a room, a few objects, the two characters, and the zoom presets as buttons.

**What's in it:**
- **The room:** a museum-style room with a red floor, beige walls and a white rail. It has 8 objects: 4 rope posts with a red rope, a plinth, a T-Rex leg, T-Rex ribs and a bench. The boy and Mom stand behind the rope.
- **Controls:** you pick what the camera looks at, either from the buttons or by clicking it in the picture. Then you set the camera's angle, height and distance, and fire any of the 9 zooms. Sliders change a zoom's strength and speed.
- **The camera:** it uses the reference's lens, 36.1 mm in a 9:16 frame.

**Checked:** I tested it in the browser. Firing Z4 (the +290% dolly in) took the camera from 3.00 m to 0.77 m, which matches the preset's size change. There are no console errors. `./verify.sh` passes: the 9 presets hit their measured sizes, and the scene check confirms nothing overlaps or sits outside the room.

**Not done:**
- **Placeholders:** the characters are simple blocks without faces, and the lighting is rough.
- **No gameplay yet:** there is no script or timeline, no scoring, and only zoom presets so far, with no whip or orbit buttons.
- **Not committed or deployed:** I haven't committed or published anything.

**The whip-tail render for the main clip** is still running on a new pod, and I'll send it when it lands.

Agreed that it looks average: blocky characters with no faces, flat orange lighting, and a panel of sliders that makes it feel like a tool, not a game.

🔴 **NEEDED:** how would you set it up? Tell me your version and I'll build that instead.

If you want my options first, here are two:
- **Film-set mode:** the scene is lit like the real clip and fills the screen, with the zooms as big buttons on the bottom edge, like a camera app. The time counts down, and the script lines show up as the beats to hit.
- **Director's desk:** the scene sits on top and the 12.6 s timeline sits below it. You drag zooms onto the timeline, then press play and watch your cut.

**My pick is film-set mode,** with the real characters exported from Blender, so it looks like the finished clip, not a prototype.

### CLAUDE 2026-11-15T12:58

Understood. That changes what we build: the tool lives inside the animators' own scenes. When they finish animating, the zoom presets are right there on their camera, and they add them as they like. I'm assuming they animate in Blender, since the presets already come from Blender code.

**What that means for the plan:**
- **An add-on, not a separate toy:** it would be a panel in their Blender scene. They pick what the camera looks at, pick a preset, and it writes the zoom as camera keyframes at the current frame, with strength and speed dials. The 9 zoom presets are first, then whips and orbits.
- **The game on top:** the scoring against a reference clip becomes a practice mode inside the same panel, and the browser room becomes optional.
- **What exists:** `recipes/zoom_presets.py` and `camera_moves.py` already produce the camera states per frame, so the add-on mostly wraps them.
- **Next step:** build the add-on and test it on the pod's Blender. My guess is about an hour for a first version that adds one zoom, though I haven't timed anything like it.

**The whip-tail render, just sent for your review:**
- **Better than before:** the picture-change rhythm correlation went from 0.05 to 0.30, and the median change from 1.44 to 3.06 (the reference is 11.6).
- **Still not good:**
  - It has 22 hard jumps, against 15 in the plain preset render.
  - The whips can sweep across an empty wall. At frame 66 the camera looks at bare wall before shot 3 appears.
  - Mom's shot, frames 60 to 63, is static where the reference has small movement.

🔴 **NEEDED:** should I start the Blender add-on now? **My pick is yes.**

"""Scene setup, materials and a studio backdrop."""
import math

import bpy


def new_scene():
    """Start from an empty file and return its scene."""
    bpy.ops.wm.read_factory_settings(use_empty=True)
    return bpy.context.scene


def principled(name, color, roughness=0.5, metallic=0.0):
    """Material with a Principled BSDF. Finds the node by type, not by its display name."""
    mat = bpy.data.materials.new(name)
    if mat.node_tree is None:  # Blender 4.x; 5.x creates the node tree by default
        mat.use_nodes = True
    bsdf = next(n for n in mat.node_tree.nodes if n.type == "BSDF_PRINCIPLED")
    bsdf.inputs["Base Color"].default_value = (*color, 1.0)
    bsdf.inputs["Roughness"].default_value = roughness
    bsdf.inputs["Metallic"].default_value = metallic
    return mat


def empty_at(scene, name, location):
    """Invisible marker object, useful as an aim point for cameras and lights."""
    obj = bpy.data.objects.new(name, None)
    obj.location = location
    scene.collection.objects.link(obj)
    return obj


def azimuth(from_point, to_point):
    """Compass angle (radians, in the XY plane) of to_point as seen from from_point."""
    return math.atan2(to_point[1] - from_point[1], to_point[0] - from_point[0])


def backdrop(scene, camera_location, target_location=(0, 0, 0), width=40.0, front=14.0,
             back=7.0, height=12.0, radius=4.0, color=(0.5, 0.5, 0.52)):
    """Studio sweep: a floor that curves up into a wall behind the subject.

- **What's in the room:** the T-Rex skeleton stands on a big grey platform, with a long tail of spheres. The red rope runs along the platform's edge. The boy and Mom stand at the rope, and the black pedestals hold the eggs.

### CLAUDE 2026-11-15T13:13

I found six free T-Rex models on Sketchfab. The previews are on your screen, and nothing is downloaded yet.

**My pick is A, "Tyrannosaurus Rex skeleton" by Rigsters.** It is a real museum-style skeleton with a detailed skull, ribs and legs, and it is the most-liked model in the search, with 1,092 likes. It has 250,518 faces, which is fine for our renders.
- **B (Scotty, 42k faces):** a fleshed-out dinosaur with skin, not a skeleton, so it doesn't match the museum piece.
- **C (marcllobet, 64k faces):** a plain white skeleton, a decent second choice.
- **D, E and F:** too small, a stylised look, or fleshed out.
- **Licence:** all six are CC-BY, so we owe the creator a credit if the clip goes public.

**How it gets in:**
- **Download:** the Blender search tool I'd normally use can't connect, because Blender doesn't run on the Mac. Downloading from Sketchfab also needs a login, so please save the glTF for A into `~/Projects/blender-funnel/assets/dino/`. I won't handle any tokens.
- **Fitting it in:** I'd then replace the T-Rex leg and ribs with it, scaled to the platform. The shots that look at the foot and leg (shots 5 to 11) are aimed at the old leg's position, so I'd re-aim them at the new foot.

🔴 **NEEDED:** A, or another letter? I'll start the moment the file is in the folder.

Env: GLB (path), OUT (folder for the pictures).\"\"\"\nimport math\nimport os\n\nimport bpy\nfrom mathutils import Vector\n\nGLB, OUT = os.environ[\"GLB\"], os.environ[\"OUT\"]\nos.makedirs(OUT, exist_ok=True)\nbpy.ops.wm.read_factory_settings(use_empty=True)\nbpy.ops.import_scene.gltf(filepath=GLB)\nsc = bpy.context.scene\nms = [o for o in sc.objects if o.type == \"MESH\"]\npts = [o.matrix_world @ Vector(c) for o in ms for c in o.bound_box]\nmn = Vector((min(p.x for p in pts), min(p.y for p in pts), min(p.z for p in pts)))\nmx = Vector((max(p.x for p in pts), max(p.y for p in pts), max(p.z for p in pts)))\nprint(\"DINO meshes\", len(ms), \"faces\", sum(len(o.data.polygons) for o in ms))\nprint(\"DINO bbox min\", tuple(round(v, 3) for v in mn), \"max\", tuple(round(v, 3) for v in mx), \"dims\", tuple(round(v, 3) for v in (mx - mn)))\nprint(\"DINO first names\", [o.name for o in ms[:45]])\n# where is the mass along each axis: vertex counts in 10 slices (the skull end is dense, the tail end thin)\nverts = [o.matrix_world @ v.co for o in ms for v in o.data.vertices]\nfor ax, nm in enumerate(\"xyz\"):\n    lo, hi = mn[ax], mx[ax]\n    cnt = [0] * 10\n    for p in verts:\n        cnt[min(9, int((p[ax] - lo) / max(hi - lo, 1e-9) * 10))] += 1\n    print(f\"DINO slices along {nm}:\", cnt)\n# clay + three views\nclay = bpy.data.materials.new(\"clay\")\nclay.diffuse_color = (0.8, 0.78, 0.72, 1)\nfor o in ms:\n    o.data.materials.clear()\n    o.data.materials.append(clay)\nc = (mn + mx) / 2\nR = max(mx - mn) * 0.62\nsun = bpy.data.objects.new(\"sun\", bpy.data.lights.new(\"sun\", \"SUN\"))\nsun.data.energy = 3.0\nsun.rotation_euler = (math.radians(50), 0, math.radians(30))\nsc.collection.objects.link(sun)\nw = bpy.data.worlds.new(\"w\")\nw.use_nodes = True\nw.node_tree.nodes[\"Background\"].inputs[\"Color\"].default_value = (0.35, 0.35, 0.38, 1)\nsc.world = w\nfrom recipes import render  # noqa: E402\nimport sys  # noqa: E402\nrender.cycles_gpu(sc, 800, 600, 16)\nfor nm, d in ((\"from_plus_x\", (1, 0, 0)), (\"from_plus_y\", (0, 1, 0)), (\"from_plus_z_top\", (0, 0.001, 1))):\n    cam = bpy.data.objects.new(nm, bpy.data.cameras.new(nm))\n    cam.data.type, cam.data.ortho_scale = \"ORTHO\", max(mx - mn) * 1.15\n    cam.location = c + Vector(d).normalized() * (R * 3)\n    cam.rotation_euler = (c - cam.location).to_track_quat(\"-Z\", \"Y\").to_euler()\n    sc.collection.objects.link(cam)\n    sc.camera = cam\n    sc.render.filepath = os.path.join(OUT, nm + \".png\")\n    bpy.ops.render.render(write_still=True)\n    print(\"DINO rendered\", nm)\nE\npython3 -m py_compile tools/inspect_glb.py && echo compiled\nsed `

### CLAUDE 2026-11-15T13:26

Model C is downloaded and checked. It's saved in `assets/dino/` as a 2.4 MB glTF, licensed CC-BY-4.0 by marcllobet, and I used your token only for that one request.

- **Token:** I didn't write it to any file or to memory. Because it now sits in this chat, I'd suggest regenerating it in your Sketchfab settings when we're done.
- **Now running:** a short inspection run on the pod. It measures the model's size, finds which way the head points, and renders clay views from the side, front and top. That tells me how to rotate and scale it onto the platform.
- **Next, once it's back:** I'll swap out the old stand-in dinosaur, give the bones the dark colour, and re-aim the foot shots.

I'll send the clay views and the measurements as soon as they land.

### CLAUDE 2026-11-15T13:33

The full-clip render with the new dinosaur failed after 23 seconds, and I've re-run it. My pod script's `W` variable, the path of the Python environment, clashed with the render width. Nothing was rendered, and the cost was a few cents for the pod to start. It should now take about 6 minutes, and I'll send the full-clip video as soon as it's back.


### USER 2026-11-15T13:37

why did it fail?


### CLAUDE 2026-11-15T13:37

It failed because of a mistake in my own pod script: two different things were both called `W`.

- **The clash:** the script uses `W` as the folder of the Python environment on the pod, `/root/funnel`. When I made the render size adjustable, I also read the render width from `W`. The earlier runs of that kind happened to set `W=1280` themselves, so it worked. This run didn't, so the script tried to use `/root/funnel` as a width and Python stopped.
- **The cost:** 23 seconds and a few cents for the pod to start. Nothing rendered.
- **The fix:** I've set the width, height and samples explicitly for the re-run. I've also renamed the folder variable in the script so it can't clash again.

It's the same weakness I described earlier: nothing tests the launch line before the pod runs it, so a small slip costs a round trip. I can add a quick local test of the script's settings before it goes to the pod, if you'd like.
